# 07 第七章分析报告：Aesthetics of Unreality

## L801 一、章节定位与功能

### L802 1.1 定位

第七章「Aesthetics of Unreality」是全书的"美学宣言"章节。如果说前六章主要处理思辨设计的"为什么"（why）、"是什么"（what）和"怎么做"（how），那么本章处理的则是思辨设计的"长什么样"（what does it look like）和"感觉如何"（what does it feel like）。这是全书中视觉案例最丰富、跨媒介引用最密集的一章。

### L803 1.2 在全书结构中的位置

本章是"美学与呈现"板块的第一章。它与第8章（传播与展览）共同构建了思辨设计的"输出端"——一旦我们制造了思辨道具（第6章），它们如何被观看、感受和传播就成了关键问题。作者明确表达了对"communication"简化为"transmitting meaning to a passive viewer"的不满，主张以"ambiguity, surprise, poetic and subtle"的方式处理现实与非现实的关系。

---

## L804 二、结构分析

### L805 2.1 章节内部结构

本章是全书最长的章节之一，可分为七个段落：

L806 **段落一**（开篇至"BEYOND PARODY, PASTICHE, AND CLICHE"）：提出核心问题——"How do you design for unreality, and what should it look like?"——确立"同时跨越现实与非现实"的美学目标。

L807 **段落二**（"AESTHETICS AND SPECULATION"）：讨论科幻插图（Syd Mead, Chris Foss）和CGI的美学传统及其局限。指出"the software is the voice behind the imagery"——工具决定了风格。

L808 **段落三**（"BEYOND PARODY"及其后续各媒介巡览）：从插画（John Hejduk, Paul Noble, Marcel Dzama, Yuichi Yokoyama, Luigi Serafini的Codex Seraphinianus）到建筑（Sottsass的Planet as Festival），再到摄影（Filip Dujardin, Josef Schulz, Institute of Critical Zoologists）——建立一个跨越媒介的"非现实美学谱系"。

L809 **段落四**（"MODEL AESTHETICS"）：聚焦"模型美学"——Thomas Demand的纸板摄影、Dunne & Raby的抽象机器人、PostlerFerguson的木质模型、Toran/Kular/Jones的The MacGuffin Library、El Ultimo Grito的Imaginary Architectures——以及"道具美学回归现实"的案例（Van Der Poll的Modular Car, Joey Ruiter的Moto Undone）。

L810 **段落五**（"CHARACTER AS PROP"与"ATMOSPHERES OF STRANGENESS"）：将美学分析从物扩展到角色（Mariko Mori, Juha Arvid Helminen, Sputniko）和氛围（nonplaces, tweaked everyday, constructed unrealities）。

L811 **段落六**（"HIDDEN REALITIES"）：讨论"现实比虚构更陌生"的摄影传统——Lars Tunbjork, Lynne Cohen, Taryn Simon, Richard Ross, Lucinda Devlin, Mike Mandel & Larry Sultan的Evidence——作为思辨美学的"原型图像"。

L812 **段落七**（结语）：总结"a world of aesthetic and communicative possibility becomes available for speculative design"。

---

## L813 三、内容分析

### L814 3.1 核心论题

**论题一：非现实美学需要在"真实"与"非真实"之间取得平衡。** "How, in a design, can you simultaneously capture the real and not-real? This is where the aesthetic challenge for speculative design lies, in successfully straddling both."

**论题二：现实主义不是唯一的可能性。** 思辨设计不应只是"看起来像真的"——"It is not a case of mimicking other design languages but the language of everyday design"。CGI的过度使用导致了一种风格上的停滞——"it is very difficult for an artist, designer, or architect to transcend the dominant style."

**论题三：媒介本身即信息。** 手绘草图立即传达思辨性——"Sketches may seem a bit old-fashioned but they immediately signal the speculative nature of the story-world"。相比之下，CGI和超写实主义可能混淆虚构与现实的边界。

**论题四：模型/道具美学作为思辨设计的核心视觉语言。** Thomas Demand的纸板模型摄影被奉为典范——"the quality of objects in his photographs epitomizes the aesthetic potential of models and their ability to straddle multiple realities—fictional and actual."

**论题五：角色和氛围也是思辨美学的构成要素。** 从单纯的"物"扩展到角色（通过服装、姿态、表情传达世界观）和氛围（nonspace, tweaked everyday, constructed unreality），构成了思辨设计的完形美学。

**论题六：隐藏现实是思辨美学的灵感来源。** 极端的制度性空间（死刑室、实验室、研究档案）因其内在的异质性而具有"proto-speculative"的美学价值——"They seem to belong in a parallel world where extreme aspects of our own world have somehow metamorphosed into whole environments."

### L815 3.2 关键案例

L816 **Syd Mead 和 Chris Foss**：1970-80年代科幻插画传统——既是灵感来源也是需要超越的视觉范式。

L817 **John Hejduk, Vladivostok sketchbook (1989)**：手绘草图作为思辨建筑的媒介——"immediately signal the speculative nature of the story-world he has created"。

L818 **Luigi Serafini, Codex Seraphinianus (1981)**：以虚构语言书写的虚构世界百科全书——"wildly speculative and impenetrable but this is its charm"。

L819 **Thomas Demand, Poll (2001)**：纸板模型摄影——"the quality of objects in his photographs epitomizes the aesthetic potential of models"。

L820 **Dunne & Raby, Technological Dreams No. 1: Robots (2007)**：抽象化、最少量细节的机器人——"We did not want people to fuss about functionality so we developed abstract forms with minimal details intended to function as prompts"。

L821 **El Ultimo Grito, Imaginary Architectures (2011)**：玻璃模型——"imprecise, evocative, metaphorical, sensual anchors for a poetic vision of how a city could be"。

L822 **Marijn Van Der Poll, Modular Car (2002)** 和 **Joey Ruiter, Moto Undone (2011)**："道具美学回归现实"——前者是可驾驶的泡沫块，后者是将摩托车剥离至纯粹反光表面的"纯粹通用交通"。

L823 **Sputniko, Menstruation Machine: Takashi's Take (2010)**：角色作为道具——通过一个渴望体验月经的男性角色来批判消费技术中的性别假设。

L824 **Lars von Trier, Dogville (2003)**：建构的抽象空间——"an almost direct application of Bertolt Brecht's principles of alienation to cinema"。

L825 **Lucinda Devlin, The Omega Suites (1991-98)**：死刑室摄影——"They are clinical, easy-to-maintain, brutally engineered environments designed to achieve one purpose—the humane killing of human beings"。

L826 **Mike Mandel & Larry Sultan, Evidence (1977)**：从研究档案中收集的无说明照片——"They compel us to interpret what is going on, to project stories and meanings onto them, illustrating that even objective records are rarely neutral"。

---

## L827 四、逻辑梳理

### L828 4.1 论证链条

> L829 **问题**：非现实应该长什么样？思辨设计如何在不牺牲智识深度的前提下吸引观众？
> L830 **批判**：戏仿（parody）和仿作（pastiche）是常见陷阱——设计师过于努力使思辨"看起来像真的"。
> L831 **探索**：拒绝现实主义霸权，拥抱"媒介多样性"——从手绘草图到CGI到摄影到模型到装置，每种媒介都提供不同的"真实性效果"。
> L832 **聚焦**：模型/道具美学——在"太像真的"（混淆虚构与现实）和"太像假的"（沦为玩具）之间找到精确的中间点。
> L833 **扩展**：美学不仅是物的视觉外观，还包括角色、氛围（空间与情调）和"隐藏现实"的灵感价值。
> L834 **结论**：思辨设计的美学自由来自对自身"非现实性"的拥抱——"designers will need to accept the fictional nature of design speculations and, rather than trying to convince the viewer that their ideas are 'real,' learn to enjoy the unreality of speculation and the aesthetic opportunities it creates."

---

## L835 五、材料使用方式

### L836 5.1 跨媒介采样

本章是全书视觉文化引用最密集的章节，涉及：科幻插画（Syd Mead, Chris Foss）、漫画（Yuichi Yokoyama）、建筑草图（John Hejduk, Ettore Sottsass）、纯艺绘画（Paul Noble, Marcel Dzama）、摄影（Filip Dujardin, Josef Schulz, Lynne Cohen, Lars Tunbjork, Taryn Simon, Richard Ross, Lucinda Devlin, Mike Mandel & Larry Sultan）、动画（Fantastic Planet）、电影（Cube, Dogville, District 9, Children of Men, Never Let Me Go）、时尚摄影（Steven Meisel, Guy Bourdin）、CGI/广告（Superflux, Roy Castle Lung Cancer Foundation）。

### L837 5.2 自我参照

作者将自己的作品（Technological Dreams Robots, Foragers）作为模型美学的案例，体现了理论与实践的统一。

### L838 5.3 摄影史中的隐藏现实传统

本章对"隐藏现实"摄影传统的详细梳理（从Lynne Cohen到Mike Mandel & Larry Sultan）是全书最具启发性的章节之一——它建立了一种"现实本身即为思辨"的美学立场。

---

## L839 六、论辩与阐述方法

### L840 6.1 美学谱系学

本章使用"谱系学"方法——追溯不同媒介中非现实美学的历史演化——而非建立单一的美学标准。

### L841 6.2 范例法

本章的论证高度依赖视觉范例——每个美学主张都通过2-3个具体的视觉案例来展示。这使得本章具有"视觉课程"的特征。

### L842 6.3 两极之间的中间道路

本章的结构逻辑是不断在两种极端之间寻找中间点：手绘 vs. CGI、可信 vs. 或信、玩具 vs. 产品、现实主义 vs. 超现实主义、白色立方 vs. 日常空间。

---

## L843 七、语言文风

### L844 7.1 代表性原文摘录

L845 核心美学挑战：
> "How do you design for unreality, and what should it look like? How should the unreal, parallel, impossible, unknown, and yet-to-exist be represented? And how, in a design, can you simultaneously capture the real and not-real?"

L846 CGI的工具决定论：
> "The tools dominate the look and feel of the imagery they are used to create and it is very difficult for an artist, designer, or architect to transcend the dominant style. In a way, the software is the voice behind the imagery."

L847 手绘草图的思辨信号：
> "Sketches may seem a bit old-fashioned but they immediately signal the speculative nature of the story-world."

L848 模型美学的典范：
> "The quality of objects in his photographs epitomizes the aesthetic potential of models and their ability to straddle multiple realities—fictional and actual."

L849 思辨的根本差异：
> "We are more interested in using fictional designs to suggest things can be very different indeed, consequently our fictions are glitchy, strange, disruptive, and hint at other places, times, and values."

L850 超写实主义的陷阱：
> "Too many visionary schemes suffer from an excess of realism that invites pragmatic critique undermining their inherent poetry and power to inspire."

L851 隐藏现实的思辨价值：
> "They seem to belong in a parallel world where extreme aspects of our own world have somehow metamorphosed into whole environments. In some cases, we are shocked to discover they are in fact from our own world. For us, these are the proto-images for an aesthetics of speculation."

L852 最终美学立场：
> "By moving away from the polar extremes of futurism and naturalism, a world of aesthetic and communicative possibility becomes available for speculative design."

---

## L901 九、与前后章关联

### L902 9.1 与第6章的关联

第6章建立了思辨道具的本体论（它们是什么；它们如何通过"拟信"运作）；第7章则追问"它们应该长什么样"。第6章结尾的"设计声音"理论在第7章中通过跨媒介的视觉谱系学获得具体展开。第6章的核心区分——"可信"vs."或信"——在第7章中成为评判不同美学策略的标准。

### L903 9.2 与第8章的关联

第7章讨论的是"作品内部的美学"（视觉语言、材料、形式）；第8章转向"作品外部的美学"（展览设计、传播策略、观众关系）。第7章的"模型/道具美学"在第8章中被置于具体的展览空间中——如"What If..."展览的Wunderkammer策略。

### L904 9.3 与第5章的关联

第5章讨论的"虚构世界"建构在第7章中获得了视觉维度的充实。第5章中Luigi Serafini的Codex Seraphinianus既是一种虚构世界建构方法（第5章），也是非现实美学的极端案例（第7章）。

## FILE `分析报告\08_Between_Reality_and_Impossible.txt`

- category: `chapter_or_full_report`
- sha256: `cee398c85cf9966f37f92bfdc081bc3ded1465b4b6522362ad84618b6b30ccdb`
- characters: 22339

## BETWEEN REALITY AND THE IMPOSSIBLE Speculative designs depend on dissemination and engagement with a public or expert audience; they are designed to circulate. The usual channels are exhibitions, publications, press, and the Internet. Each channel or medium creates its own issues of accessibility, elitism, populism, sophistication, audience, and so on. This need for dissemination means speculative designs have to be striking but a danger is they end up being little more than visual icons for communicating an idea, in an instant. The best speculative designs do more than communicate; they suggest possible uses, interactions, and behaviors not always obvious at a quick glance. ## CONCEPTUAL WINDOW SHOPPERS So far, the exhibition has been the main platform for us. Although projects might have developed in different contexts, the exhibition serves as a form of reporting space for presenting the results of research and experimentation. Design exhibitions usually show existing products, survey historical movements, or celebrate heroes and superstars but they could also serve other purposes; they could function as spaces for critical reflection. Collections such as those of the Wellcome Trust, Pitt Rivers Museum, or London Museum hold everyday objects from distant societies, either in time or geography. When we see a strange shoe or ritualistic object we wonder what kind of society must have produced it, how it was structured, what values, beliefs, and dreams motivated it, if it was wealthy or poor. We enact a form of window shopping, trying things out in our minds. It requires imaginative effort but it also provides scope for individual interpretation. If, rather than looking back in time, we presented people with fictional artifacts from alternative versions of our own society or its possible future, would people begin to relate to them in the same way-a sort of speculative material culture, fictional archeology, or imaginary anthropology? For many designers, there is a natural tendency to view museums and galleries in a negative light as inaccessible and elitist places to show design. But today, there are many different kinds of museums and galleries, each serving different purposes and catering to different audiences-specialist design museums, science museums, national museums, regional art centers, private galleries-each offering very different opportunities for designers and the public. Early on, we did everything we could to avoid exhibiting work in a white cube and everything it stood for. We showed in shop windows, homes, shopping centers, cafes, gardens. But always, the work became about the space itself, or the context, rather than the ideas we wished to explore. Slowly, we began to view museums and galleries as experimental spaces, test sites, places for reporting back on our experiments and sharing the results, sometimes with designers, sometimes with broader publics. They can complement other media channels, offering more aesthetic, intimate, unmediated, and contemplative forms of engagement with issues and ideas. Since the early 2000s, the number of channels for disseminating designs through different media has exploded: from exhibitions and magazine reviews to YouTube and vimeo channels, Twitter, personal websites, blogs, and so on. Hiromi Ozaki's Menstruation Machine: Takashi's Take was first exhibited in the RCA degree show in 2010, which had twenty thousand visitors. It was picked up by Wired online and after this appeared on many specialized technology and media art blogs where it was discussed and critiqued in some depth. It was extensively tweeted and began to receive major but superficial exposure, often oversimplified and sensationalized. After appearing as an artwork in an exhibition at the Tokyo Museum of Art, it was covered in newspapers, art magazines, books, and catalogs. In 2011 it was shown in the Talk to Me exhibition at MoMA as design. In this setting it challenged designers and professionals as much as the public. All this time it also existed as a YouTube video aimed at teenagers.' From its first showing at the RCA as a student project it consistently moved from high to low culture, from pop to niche, through art, design, and technology contexts. Throughout the project, the designer made full use of social media such as Twitter and Facebook not only to promote the project but also to seek volunteers, collaborators, and assistance. It's impossible to say where this project sits based on where it has been shown-artwork, design proposal, high culture, pop, teen sensation? It is all these things to different audiences based on where and how they encounter it. ## SPECULATIVE WUNDERKAMMER Positioning a speculative design is always an issue. Many people struggle to know what they are looking at and how they should relate to it-film prop, science fiction, product design, art? This is something we experimented with in an exhibition we curated with Michael John Gorman for the Science Gallery in Dublin in 2009. We used the title, "What If..." to frame each exhibit as a question, something to enjoy, a prop for speculating on very specific topics. The exhibition invited visitors to step out of their everyday reality and enjoy engaging with a variety of what-if questions in the form of tangible design proposals. The exhibition consisted of twenty-nine projects exploring the social, cultural, and ethical implications of different areas of science. Most of the projects were graduating MA projects from the Design Interactions program at the RCA done in consultation with scientists. Questions included, What if human tissue could be used to make objects? What if everyday products contained synthetically produced living components? What if we could evaluate the genetic potential of lovers? What if we could use smell to find the perfect partner? And, what if our emotions could be read by machines? Although aimed very much at adults and almost completely noninteractive, it was a surprising success with schoolchildren and their teachers because it provided an opportunity to debate issues concerning the science they were learning about in school. One of the exhibits, The Cloud Project (2009) by Catherine Kramer and Zoe Papadopoulou, offered a platform for discussing nanotechnology. The designers were interested in debates surrounding the disposal of products that use nanotechnology and whether nanoparticles are a health hazard in the way asbestos is. Early in the research they discovered that chefs sometimes use liquid nitrogen to make ice cream, which modifies it at a molecular level, affecting the sensation of eating it. For the designers, this was nano ice cream. From this they had the idea of using an ice cream van as a mobile platform for talks and presentations by activists, scientists, and others. During the project they consulted many scientists including Richard Jones, a nanoscientist with an interest in geo-engineering. Together they realized that, in theory at least, clouds could be seeded to make flavored snow based on a modified version of established cloud seeding technology. Out of this the Cloud Project was born, an ice cream van for seeding clouds to produce flavored snow. At this point the technology exceeded what was possible in an MA project, technically and financially, so the cloud-seeding canons were nonworking. ![](images/2b5e3794c03e7a58066b2b487ef5fdd414d9a814103f6907d2c1190752f65bf6.jpg) Catherine Kramer and Zoe Papadopoulou, The Cloud Project, 2009. The ice cream van has appeared in several science festivals around the world, each time guests are invited to make presentations while the audience sample different forms of technologically modified ice cream including fizzy carbon dioxide bubbles, liquid nitrogen ice cream, and flavored vapors inhaled rather than eaten. The audience experiences tasty, technologically modified food while listening to and participating in discussions about possible health implications for nanotechnology. ![](images/de20071778a6a80a6e9bad41164958b995ae67753f5e767e97ab10bccc77f249.jpg) Dunne & Raby, What If? 2010-2011, Wellcome Trust Windows, London. Photograph Dunne & Raby. Using what-if questions was an approach we developed further for the Wellcome Trust Windows in 2010-2011. Here exhibits were selected based on strong visual impact, intrigue, and ability to arouse curiosity in passersby viewed at different distances-on the foot path, from passing busses, and while waiting at a nearby bus stop. We developed a third variation for the first Beijing International Design Triennial in 2011, which juxtaposed large images and videos with smaller objects displayed on tables. All three exhibitions acted as speculative Wunderkammer, cabinets of curiosity on the scale of rooms, presenting the exhibits as imaginary anthropology rather than art or product design. ![](images/6412f4035e3731e5693bd88088f0d04cb07b729d7de7550932925a4b1cb208bc.jpg) Dunne & Raby, What If? 2010-2011, Wellcome Trust Windows, London. Photograph Kellenberger - White. ![](images/25de3efba1d93fb6778250c61ec64b904731f610b3213f637cadeeb9c2dbd3c5.jpg) Dunne & Raby, What If? Beijing International Design Triennial, 2011. IMAGINARY INSTITUTIONS For the EPSRC Impact! project (2010) we worked with the EPSRC (Engineering and Physical Sciences Research Council) to match sixteen designers to sixteen research projects they were funding.2 Projects in the exhibition ranged from renewable energy devices and security technologies to the emerging fields of synthetic biology and quantum computing. Rather than simply focusing on applications for the science research, designers explored the social, ethical, and political implications of the research. ![](images/a86a2df2ee848f6d975fa594a2e77d7ed6b740639c403df7048aeef305fb0745.jpg) Design Interactions (RCA), EPSRC Impact! project and exhibition, 2010. Photograph by Sylvian Deleu. Despite being in a gallery, the exhibition played down the "white cube" setting and referenced institutional open days. Spread out on tables, visitors could enjoy outcomes from a wide range of strange interdisciplinary research projects. Using tables rather than plinths, low-hanging ceiling lights rather than spotlights, carpeted floors, and painted lower walls the exhibition design alluded to an imaginary research institute. The projects brought a new perspective to the research by offering creative interpretations of these new scientific ideas. In Phantom Recorder (2010) by Revital Cohen, a collaboration with Cambridge Centre for Brain Repair, medical research into improving the interface between a person's nervous system and a prosthetic limb served as the starting point for a more poetic application of medical technology. When a limb is lost the mind often develops a unique phantom sensation. It could be an extension, an addition, or just a replacement. The technology being researched by the Cambridge team could, theoretically, allow for the sensation to be recorded and played back later, allowing the person to retain an imaginary but important sensation. When the prosthetic has been fitted, digital data of the recorded phantom sensation can be transmitted to the implant, allowing the nerves to re-create the sensation of the telescoped phantom hand, the third foot, or the split arm. The project applies research into a serious and difficult practical issue to more poetic needs. It shifts attention away from using medical technologies to fix our bodies to creating entirely new sensations and possibilities. ![](images/f6e8876549c724749ec8dae4b2cba3088eb3146f1e31e9fc0316fe1d807048c8.jpg) Revital Cohen, Phantom Recorder, 2010. ![](images/61b97f10b0a168f39da8795086a111bde663a0eae79dd16389f2036ccf5a3bc2.jpg) David Benque, Fabulous Fabbers, 2010. Photograph courtesy of EPSRC. In Fabulous Fabbers (2010) David Benque worked with a group of scientists researching extreme miniaturization and integrated manufacturing similar to rapid prototyping but with built-in electronics. Benque wondered if this technology might change the meaning of factories. Rather than being situated on the outskirts of towns, once they were free of massive machinery, they would become mobile, almost like festivals of manufacture, traveling from town to town, setting up for a few days then moving on. There would be lavish corporate tents, modest one-man kiosks, and, of course, dodgy vans parked under motorways or in car parks to satisfy the black market. ![](images/a7e78f540bf48e773fb141deb1fafb981765a663e9a465fb4d4dff2be9c6a668.jpg) Superflux (Anab Jain and John Arden), The 5th Dimensional Camera, 2010. If Fabulous Fabbers is impossibly utopian, The 5th Dimensional Camera (2010) by Anab Jain and John Arden is simply impossible. Working with scientists researching quantum mechanics, they developed a prop for a fictional camera that could take pictures of alternative universes suggested by quantum physics. They explored how people might deal with glimpses into possible parallel lives. Although the most far-fetched of all the designs produced for the exhibition, the prop has been featured in scientific publications as a concrete starting point for discussions about multiverses. Although the focus of the Impact project was an exhibition, it highlighted different ways design and science could interact: design for science, when design is used to communicate or illustrate the research; design with science, when it is a true collaboration between designer and scientist; design through science, when the designer does some science; and design about science, when issues and implications arising from the research are explored through design, and which is the most interesting approach for us. In several cases designers identified possible negative implications for the scientist's research but because of the trust they had shared felt it would be wrong to pursue these darker possibilities. For us, the most promising model is when a topic is explored in consultation with several scientists in order to maintain critical distance. ## CHAIN REACTIONS We are very interested in how the relationship between the reality of the here-and-now and the fictional worlds alluded to through props, atmosphere, supporting material, staging, and so on can be managed. In Between Reality and the Impossible for the Saint Etienne International Design Biennale in 2010 we were able to explore this thoroughly because we could design everything from the exhibits to the scenography. We started with ideas that we translated into designs then worked with writer Alex Burrett and photographer Jason Evans to develop scenarios through written vignettes and photographs. Everything was given equal importance in the exhibition; photos and texts were presented as three-dimensional objects beside the designs. The intention was to create a chain reaction starting from our initial thoughts and ideas through the objects, Evans's images, and Burrett's texts to be developed further in the imaginations of visitors and reports in other media. It consisted of four projects: Designs for an Overpopulated Planet, No. 1: Foragers; Stop and Scan; EM Listeners; and AfterLife. We will focus on Foragers.3 ![](images/909f2c072c5eff5fce1b9fb778870a6c41e0ad5ab810b692abb70c65c80f9f8f.jpg) Dunne & Raby, Between Reality and the Impossible for the Saint Etienne International Design Biennale, 2010. Photograph by Dunne & Raby. ![](images/a9ff400cc751b30103f585227d6e83f0e6a1f2966a01376e59a3b4eedcdd1c42.jpg) Dunne & Raby, Designs for an Overpopulated Planet, No. 1: Foragers, 2009. Video still. ![](images/5624e5846abc442acb92fd8917d1491f25ee544f7a55e5baff9b1602dc1debee.jpg) Dunne & Raby, Designs for an Overpopulated Planet, No. 1: Foragers, 2009. Video still. ## FORAGERS The starting point for this project was a brief from Design Indaba exploring the future of farming in the face of food shortages. According to the UN we need to produce 70 percent more food in the next forty years. Yet we continue to overpopulate the planet, use up resources, and ignore all the warning signs. The current situation is completely unsustainable. In 2050 the UN predicts that the world population will be nine billion. Based on the assumption that governments and industry together will not solve the problem, and that groups of people will need to use available knowledge to build their own solutions, bottom-up, we looked at evolutionary processes and molecular technologies4 to explore how we could take control or our own evolution. What if it were possible to extract nutritional value from nonhuman foods using a combination of synthetic biology5 and new digestive devices inspired by the digestive systems of other mammals, birds, fish, and insects? Foragers builds on existing groups of people working at the edges of society who may initially appear extreme-guerrilla gardeners, garage biologists, amateur horticulturalists, and foragers. Inspired by these groups we imagined a group of people take their fate into their own hands and begin building devices that function as external digestive systems. They use synthetic biology to create microbial stomach bacteria and mechanical devices to maximize the nutritional value of the urban environment, making up for any shortcomings in the increasingly limited diet available commercially. These people are the new urban foragers. When developing the objects we explored a range of points of access into the scenario for different people: from a near-future fermenting container worn around the neck to a more extreme prosthetic device that suggested possible transhumanist values. We avoided hyper-realism in the design of the objects and the photography. It was very important that they clearly signaled their unreality so that viewers were aware they were looking at ideas, not products. Rather than the foragers being grungy and dressed in obvious clothing, the photographer suggested they wear outdoor, sporty clothes to challenge expectations of them being organic and antitechnology. Alex's short stories suggested the foragers were far from idealistic, quite pragmatic, and had a complex relationship to mainstream society rather than simply being an isolated cult or commune. These different facets of the project were laid out spatially; objects, photos, and texts were given equal importance so that visitors could wander through the space piecing together their own ideas about Foragers. ## THE WAY YOU MOVE I never judge someone by their appearance. I look at the way they move - it runs far deeper. A junkie in a fine suit and Italian leather shoes still fidgets like they're itching for a fix. One of the first things that struck me about The Foragers was the way they move. Every social group has its idiosyncratic way of moving, with common postures and locomotion styles subconsciously copied by those wanting to belong. For those who live close to the land, aping is unnecessary. Their physicality is forged by their environments. Arable farmers stride as if permanently crossing freshly ploughed fields. Goat herders display balletic balance gained from negotiating steep mountainsides. Surfers adopt athletic poses: living statues infused with Neptune's might. Foragers, because they live closer to the land than any group, are the most physically in tune with their surroundings. Although human, if you blur your eyes they are beasts patrolling the Serengeti or wild creatures waltzing through a jungle. I'd previously found our kind clumsy, as if unsteadily making our way on two legs is penance for mastery of the planet. Let me tell you, these committed hobbyists have recaptured the natural grace of the animal kingdom. Story, Alex Burrett, from Between Reality and the Impossible for the Saint Etienne International Design Biennale, 2010. ![](images/d7804f8040670fdce922ea37beed0eb1b767665deb5f43b58f55e42b6ac71ed5.jpg) Dunne & Raby, from Designs for an Overpopulated Planet, No. 1: Foragers, 2010. Photograph by Jason Evans. We believe there is tremendous value and potential for design exhibitions to connect with science, not as a communication medium but for sparking discussion and debate about possible technological futures. As John Gray writes in Endgames,6 "Science and technology will serve human needs only insofar as our societies contain cultures and communities whose selfunderstanding is rich enough and deep enough to contain science and technology-and sometimes to restrain them. Of which late modern society is this true?" ![](images/a503f6c476e136801a543ca2d414a1d165cb3573dcb1f414d8c5a13375d0343e.jpg) Dunne & Raby, from Designs for an Overpopulated Planet, No. 7: Foragers, 2010. Photograph by Jason Evans. For us, the exhibition and, in particular, museum exhibitions are ideal places to explore and enrich our "self-understanding." We can build on existing conceptions of what exhibitions are and how they work to develop new approaches and presentation formats. These days, exhibitions are highly accessible. Paraphrasing Jan Boelen, artistic director of Z33, we need to build audiences rather than targeting them.' Exhibitions can bring together people interested in how design can engage with the ideas and disciplines shaping our lives, not only in science but also in fields such as politics, law, and economics. We fully agree with Paola Antonelli, senior curator of design at MoMA, when she suggests museums can become laboratories for rethinking society, places for showing not what already exists, but more important, what is yet to exist. ![](images/987db97ad996ff2c84c8994010ce6e2aca3d8ed929dda30b01a23a8c84c5aebf.jpg) Dunne & Raby, Between Reality and the Impossible for the Saint Etienne International Design Biennale, 2010. Photograph by Dunne & Raby. ![](images/f4c6d47f8f5078bfd8860f79c0aa2f8c46ae5eeed93026fa1391b994200ca20b.jpg) Dunne & Raby, from Designs for an Overpopulated Planet, No. 7: Foragers, 2010. Photograph by Jason Evans. ![](images/711fe5e1406fc392dec97cb3e10041330cc5f7e09d968ffd6d24d7ed32eb2151.jpg) Dunne & Raby, from Designs for an Overpopulated Planet, No. 1: Foragers, 2010. Photograph by Jason Evans. ![](images/3aa703cce44d3a157f9a6c46ecb2c901410e1b6c4d1e0a5fabd67655376fe8bc.jpg)

Dunne & Raby, from Designs for an Overpopulated Planet, No. 1: Foragers, 2010. Photograph by Jason Evans.

# 08 第八章分析报告：Between Reality and the Impossible

## L1001 一、章节定位与功能

### L1002 1.1 定位

第八章「Between Reality and the Impossible」是全书的"传播与展览"章节。其核心问题是：思辨设计一旦被制造出来，如何进入公共领域与观众相遇？本章是作者以策展人身份对思辨设计的传播机制进行的系统反思。

### L1003 1.2 在全书结构中的位置

本章是"美学与呈现"板块的第二章，与第7章（非现实美学）共同构成思辨设计的"输出端"。如果说第7章回答"它们应该长什么样"，第8章则回答"它们应该在哪里被看到以及被如何看到"。

---

## L1004 二、结构分析

### L1005 2.1 章节内部结构

本章可分为五个段落：

L1006 **段落一**（"CONCEPTUAL WINDOW SHOPPERS"）：讨论展览空间的功能——从"展示已有之物"到"呈现尚未存在之物"——以及Sputniko的Menstruation Machine作为跨媒介传播的案例。

L1007 **段落二**（"SPECULATIVE WUNDERKAMMER"）：作者以三次策展实践（都柏林科学画廊"What If..."、伦敦Wellcome Trust橱窗、北京国际设计三年展）展示不同的展览策略——核心概念是"思辨好奇柜"（speculative Wunderkammer）。

L1008 **段落三**（"IMAGINARY INSTITUTIONS"与"CHAIN REACTIONS"）：讨论EPSRC Impact!项目中的"虚构研究机构"展览策略，以及圣埃蒂安双年展中通过"链式反应"模型（物→照片→文本→观众想象）构建的展览叙事。

L1009 **段落四**（"FORAGERS"节）：以Foragers项目为深入研究案例，详细展示思辨设计展览中物、照片、文本三者的平等协作关系。

L1010 **段落五**（"THE WAY YOU MOVE"与结尾）：以作家Alex Burrett的配文为案例，展示文本如何与视觉/物质元素协同工作，以及博物馆作为"重新思考社会的实验室"的愿景。

---

## L1011 三、内容分析

### L1012 3.1 核心论题

**论题一：展览是思辨设计的核心传播平台。** "the exhibition serves as a form of reporting space for presenting the results of research and experimentation." 但设计展览可以超越"展示已有产品"和"庆祝英雄设计师"，成为"critical reflection"的空间。

**论题二：概念橱窗购物（conceptual window shopping）。** 当我们在博物馆看到来自遥远时空的日常物品时，我们会想象生产它的社会是什么样子。这一"想象性考古"的机制正是思辨设计展览的核心操作原理。

**论题三：思辨好奇柜（speculative Wunderkammer）。** 作者发展了三种展览策略——"What If..."（以问题框架统摄展品）、Wellcome橱窗（以视觉冲击力吸引路人）、北京三年展（图像与物件的并置）——都服务于将展览呈现为"imaginary anthropology rather than art or product design"。

**论题四：链式反应模型。** 在圣埃蒂安双年展中，作者实验了一种"链式反应"展览模型：从设计师的初始想法→设计物→Jason Evans的摄影→Alex Burrett的文字→观众的想象→其他媒体的转化报道。每个环节都同等重要。

**论题五：设计-科学合作的四种模式。** 基于EPSRC Impact!项目的经验，作者区分了：(1) 为科学设计（沟通/展示），(2) 与科学一起设计（真正合作），(3) 通过科学设计（设计师做科学），(4) 关于科学的设计（探索意涵）——第四种最有趣但也最难。

**论题六：博物馆作为实验室。** 引用Paola Antonelli的观点："museums can become laboratories for rethinking society, places for showing not what already exists, but more important, what is yet to exist."

### L1013 3.2 关键案例

L1014 **Sputniko, Menstruation Machine: Takashi's Take (2010) 的传播轨迹**：从RCA毕业展（20,000参观者）→Wired→设计/媒体艺术博客→Twitter→东京美术馆→MoMA "Talk to Me"展——展示了思辨设计从"亚文化"到"高雅文化"再回到"流行文化"的跨媒介传播循环。

L1015 **Dunne & Raby联合策展, "What If..." (2009, 都柏林科学画廊)**：29个以"假如"问题框架的展览项目，针对成年人但意外受到学童和教师的欢迎——"it provided an opportunity to debate issues concerning the science they were learning about in school。"

L1016 **Catherine Kramer & Zoe Papadopoulou, The Cloud Project (2009)**：以冰淇淋车作为纳米技术公共讨论的移动平台——通过可食用技术（液氮冰淇淋）和专家讲座的结合来讨论纳米颗粒的健康风险。

L1017 **Dunne & Raby, Wellcome Trust Windows (2010-11)**：基于"视觉冲击力、引发好奇心的能力、以及在不同距离上的可见度"来选择展品的橱窗展览。

L1018 **EPSRC Impact!项目 (2010)**：16位设计师与16个EPSRC资助科研项目配对——Revital Cohen的Phantom Recorder和David Benque的Fabulous Fabbers展示了思辨设计如何从科学中提取诗意而非仅仅是应用。

L1019 **Dunne & Raby, Designs for an Overpopulated Planet, No. 1: Foragers (2010)**：作为"链式反应"展览模型的核心案例——一个关于城市采食者利用合成生物学和消化假体的近未来场景。物件的设计刻意避免超写实主义以"clearly signaled their unreality so that viewers were aware they were looking at ideas, not products"。

---

## L1020 四、逻辑梳理

### L1021 4.1 论证链条

> L1022 **问题一**：思辨设计如何进入公共领域？
> L1023 **答案一**：展览是主要平台——但需要重新定义展览的功能。
> L1024 **问题二**：如何让观众理解他们看到的是什么（不是产品、不是艺术、不是电影道具）？
> L1025 **答案二**：策展框架至关重要——"What If..."的问题式框架、"虚构人类学"的呈现方式、"好奇柜"的展览传统——都是帮助观众"进入状态"的策略。
> L1026 **问题三**：不同的展示空间如何影响思辨设计的效果？
> L1027 **答案三**：科学画廊、基金会橱窗、设计双年展——各有不同的受众和效果。重点不是"找到正确的空间"而是发展适合不同空间的展示策略。
> L1028 **问题四**：如何让物、像、文在展览中协同而不是互相抵消？
> L1029 **答案四**："链式反应"模型——物、摄影、文字各司其职，在空间中被平等呈现，让观众"wander through the space piecing together their own ideas"。
> L1030 **结论**："We believe there is tremendous value and potential for design exhibitions to connect with science, not as a communication medium but for sparking discussion and debate."

---

## L1031 五、材料使用方式

### L1032 5.1 策展经验作为一手材料

本章最独特之处在于作者使用了自身的大量策展经验作为论证材料——三次"What If..."展览、EPSRC Impact!项目、圣埃蒂安双年展Foragers项目。

### L1033 5.2 项目案例分析

Impact!项目被作为"设计-科学合作四种模式"的分析框架来源——这是一种从实践中提炼理论的方法。

### L1034 5.3 文学/文本材料的协作使用

Alex Burrett为Foragers创作的配文被完整引述——展示了文字在思辨设计展览中不作为"说明"而作为"平行创作"的角色。

---

## L1035 六、论辩与阐述方法

### L1036 6.1 经验反思法

本章的核心论辩方法是"实践后的理论反思"——作者不是在提出抽象命题，而是在描述和分析自己已完成的展览实践。

### L1037 6.2 案例比较法

三种展览策略（都柏林/伦敦/北京）的并置比较提供了"同一原则在不同空间中的差异化实现"的生动展示。

### L1038 6.3 "展示而非讲述"

Foragers项目的详细展开——包括Burrett配文的全引——构成了一种"show, don't tell"的论证方式。

---

## L1039 七、语言文风

### L1040 7.1 代表性原文摘录

L1041 思辨消费行为：
> "When we see a strange shoe or ritualistic object we wonder what kind of society must have produced it, how it was structured, what values, beliefs, and dreams motivated it...We enact a form of window shopping, trying things out in our minds."

L1042 虚构人类学：
> "If, rather than looking back in time, we presented people with fictional artifacts from alternative versions of our own society or its possible future, would people begin to relate to them in the same way—a sort of speculative material culture, fictional archeology, or imaginary anthropology?"

L1043 Sputniko的传播轨迹：
> "It's impossible to say where this project sits based on where it has been shown—artwork, design proposal, high culture, pop, teen sensation? It is all these things to different audiences based on where and how they encounter it."

L1044 链式反应：
> "The intention was to create a chain reaction starting from our initial thoughts and ideas through the objects, Evans's images, and Burrett's texts to be developed further in the imaginations of visitors and reports in other media."

L1045 设计作为粘合剂（引用Ken Arnold）：
> "[Design can be] the stable platform on which to entertain unusual bedfellows. The glue for things that may not be naturally sticky. The lubricant that allows movement between ideas that don't quite run together. The medium through which we can make otherwise awkward connections and comparisons. The language for tricky conversations and translations."

L1046 Antonelli的博物馆愿景：
> "museums can become laboratories for rethinking society, places for showing not what already exists, but more important, what is yet to exist."

L1047 虚构研究机构美学：
> "Spread out on tables, visitors could enjoy outcomes from a wide range of strange interdisciplinary research projects. Using tables rather than plinths, low-hanging ceiling lights rather than spotlights, carpeted floors, and painted lower walls the exhibition design alluded to an imaginary research institute."

---

## L1088 九、与前后章关联

### L1089 9.1 与第7章的关联

第7章建立了"非现实美学"的视觉谱系——即思辨设计物本身应有的视觉品质；第8章则处理这些物被放置于何种空间、以何种方式被观看。第7章末尾的"隐藏现实"美学（Lynne Cohen, Lucinda Devlin）实际上预设了"观看的政治"——而这一点在第8章中被充分展开。

### L1090 9.2 与第9章的关联

第8章以Paola Antonelli的名言——"museums can become laboratories for rethinking society"——收束，这一"社会重新想象"的主题在第9章中被放大为全书的终极命题：思辨设计作为"社会梦想的催化剂"。第8章的Foragers项目（城市采食者构建自主生存系统）是第9章的United Micro Kingdoms（四个意识形态超级郡）在较小尺度上的预演。

### L1091 9.3 与第4章的关联

第4章的"设计-科学合作"讨论在第8章中被发展为更系统的四种模式分类。第4章以"科学材料的思辨使用"为核心，第8章则以"展览空间中的科学对话"为核心——两者共同构成了思辨设计在科技领域的完整实践循环。

---
